Chapter 3: The Canvas
August 2023

Desiree Washington stood back from the wall, paintbrush in hand, studying the
emerging mural with a critical eye. The morning light streamed through the tall
windows of what would soon be the safe house's community gathering space,
illuminating the vibrant colors she had been applying since dawn. The painting
depicted a stylized tree with roots that transformed into the silhouettes of people
holding hands—a visual representation of the safe house's legacy of connection and
support across generations.

She placed a hand on her growing belly, feeling the flutter of movement within. At five
months pregnant, her body was changing daily, becoming both canvas and creator in
its own right. The parallel wasn't lost on her—this building transforming under their
collective care, her body nurturing new life, both processes requiring patience and
faith in what couldn't yet be fully seen.

"That's coming along beautifully," Victor said, entering with a cup of herbal tea. He had
taken to bringing her caffeine-free options since learning of her pregnancy, a small
gesture of care that touched her deeply.

"Thanks," she replied, accepting the tea gratefully. "I'm trying to incorporate elements
from the community workshops, but maintain a cohesive vision."

Victor studied the mural, his eyes lingering on the faces emerging from the tree's roots
—faces of different ages, races, gender expressions, all connected by intertwining
branches. "You've captured it perfectly—how we're all part of the same story, even
when we don't realize it."

Desiree sipped her tea, the ginger soothing her ever-present morning sickness. "That's
what I felt when I first came here as a teenager—that I was joining something larger
than myself. I want others to feel that too, especially now when there's so much effort
to isolate and divide us."

She set down her tea and picked up her brush again, adding detail to one of the faces—
a young person with features that subtly evoked Elijah's. Though she had never
claimed supernatural abilities, Desiree had always experienced the world through a
spiritual lens inherited from her grandmother, a practitioner of Vodou traditions from
New Orleans. Since beginning work on the safe house renovations, she had felt Elijah's
presence guiding her artistic choices, suggesting colors and forms that would speak to
those who needed shelter.

"The community workshop starts at eleven," Victor reminded her. "Do you need help
setting up?"

Desiree glanced at her watch, surprised by how the morning had slipped away. "Yes,
please. I still need to prepare the supplies and arrange the tables."

As they worked together to transform the adjacent room into an art space, Desiree felt
another flutter in her belly, stronger this time. She paused, hand pressed against the
movement.

"Active today?" Victor asked with a gentle smile.

"Very," Desiree confirmed. "Especially when I'm painting. I think the rhythmic
movement soothes them."

"Them?" Victor raised an eyebrow. "You're not finding out the gender?"

Desiree shook her head. "No. And I'm using neutral pronouns until they can tell me
themselves who they are." She gave Victor a meaningful look. "Growing up in this
community, I've seen too many lives complicated by assumptions made before birth."

Victor nodded in understanding. "Elijah would approve. He always said the greatest
gift we can give children is the freedom to discover themselves."

The mention of Elijah brought a familiar ache—grief mingled with purpose. Desiree
had known him since she was sixteen, when she had run away from her aunt's home in
Tampa after her father's death. Elijah had given her not just shelter but a sense of
possibility, encouraging her art when others dismissed it as impractical. Now,
transforming his home into a community space felt like completing a circle, honoring
his faith in her while extending it to others.

"I miss him," she said simply.

"Me too," Victor replied. "Every day. But I see him in what we're building here. In what
you're creating." He gestured to the mural visible through the doorway. "He would be
proud."

The community art workshop drew a more diverse group than Desiree had
anticipated. There were teenagers from local high schools, elderly residents from the
neighborhood, members of Tampa's LGBTQ+ organizations, and even a few curious
passersby who had seen the flyers posted around Ybor City. They gathered around
tables covered in art supplies, some confident in their creative abilities, others

"Art isn't about talent," Desiree assured them as she explained the workshop's

we're creating pieces that reflect what community means to you, what safe space
looks like, what healing feels like. These will inspire the permanent installations

She demonstrated various techniques they could use—collage, painting, drawing—

An elderly Black woman was carefully constructing a collage featuring images of hands
of different ages and colors. "My church group used to meet secretly with gay men
during the AIDS crisis," she explained quietly. "When their families and other churches
turned them away, we would bring food, sit with them, hold their hands. Just touch
was revolutionary then, when people were afraid."

A teenager with a partially shaved head was creating a vibrant painting of a door
standing open in a storm. "My parents kicked me out when I came out as non-binary,"

friend's family who took me in. That's what saved me—just having a door that would
open."

A middle-aged Latina woman was drawing a detailed kitchen scene. "In my culture,

your guard. My son came out to me while we were making tamales. I told him, 'Mi hijo,
if you can make tamales this good, you can love whoever you want.'" Her laugh carried
both pain and joy. "It wasn't that simple, of course. But we started there, in that safe
space of the kitchen."

house's visual identity—hands as symbols of connection, open doors representing
welcome, kitchens as spaces of nourishment and acceptance. The participants were

resonate with meaning.

When the sickness passed, she leaned against the cool tile wall, eyes closed, waiting
